**[#203 · 2024-08-02 21:32 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

you can use both! That's what I"m playing with right now.  It's basically a smart switch that controls a contactor (relays are not safe with this much power).  This lets you schedule the miner and/or use a wifi thermometer ($10 on amazon) to shut down/turn on at preset temperatures.  The Braiins firmware lets you arrive at your desired temp. asymptotically so you can maintain uptime and improve efficiency by downclocking at you arrive at your desired temp.  The implications of this are insane for home heating.

❤1

**[#204 · 2024-08-02 21:33 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

I've got my $/kWh down from $0.14 CAD to 0.097 CAD just from scheduling the miners to shut down during peak electric rates

❤1

**[#205 · 2024-08-02 21:33 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

**[#231 · 2024-08-02 21:54 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

the thermostat controlled on/off, paired with Braiins firmware that's set to downclock by like 300 w when the chip hits 70C does something like this to the duty cycle:

**[#243 · 2024-08-02 22:00 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

it's controlled by the inlet temp, which is controlled by the wifi thermometer/switch.  As temps in the room go from 20 to 25, the chip will go from 60 - 70 (pretty reliably with the 3 models I've tested (S19K Pro, S19 XP, and S21).  Set braiins dynamic performance scaling to downclock by 300w at 70c chip temp. Presto, done.

**[#250 · 2024-08-02 22:03 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

within Braiins OS+, you can have the miner downclock when you hit a desired chip temp.  All you have to do is see what temp your chip is at when running at max power @ 25c ambient room temp.  Then set the trigger to downclock just below that number so you approach your desired room temp slowly (prioritizing uptime)

❤1

**[#427 · 2024-08-05 19:06 · Bob]**

My two cents: one factor to keep in mind is 'the coldest day'.   Matching the heating rate between the existing home heating system over a month with a miner heat over a month will give you the average heat needed.   If you look at that every month, that will give you a good target for a miner system size.   However, you have to also think about extremes within months.

For a real example, I replaced my 120,000 BTU (35kW) furnace with my 2 S19jPros, that can overclock to 9kW when needed.  These worked great over the entire winter...my house was a constant 71-72 F 24/7...NICE!   That worked well...except for 'the coldest day'...actually 2 of them.   We got down to below 0 for a couple 24 hour periods and the miners couldn't keep up...I needed to turn on my nat. gas fireplace...which was fine for me (warm fire on a cold day is pretty cool!), but showed a weakness in my design.

When you look at your customer's existing system, the size was set for a specific reason...'the coldest day'.   The rest of the year it's oversized, and that was intentional.  I think that's the challenge with replacing an existing heater with miners.   If you design a miner system for the coldest day, you're going to spend a lot of money on mining power that won't get used...but if you don't design for the coldest day and the customer doesn't have supplementary heating,...they will get cold and unhappy 😞.

It may be a good idea to add a supplementary heater (non-mining) that kicks on during those 1-2 coldest days.   If you make it cheap enough, the overall system will still be better than dumb electrical heating.  Thoughts?

👍2
🔥1

**[#449 · 2024-08-06 21:42 · Travis Bitkle]**

Jumping in late here. The PDUs take three phase, but the individual PSUs on the miners take 200-250v single phase. The PDUs divide up the three hot legs.

**[#451 · 2024-08-06 21:43 · Travis Bitkle]**

L1 feeds the first 8 C19 plugs, L2 the second batch of 8 plugs, and L3 the remaining 8 plugs.

🤯1

**[#669 · 2024-08-19 12:45 · Dane O]**

I think the real problem is the closer the two liquids come to equal temperature (lower delta T) between the inlet temp and desired outlet temp of the "heated" medium you need A LOT larger heat transfer area... So this thing being able to put out 80c is a great start for 60c desired domestic hot water temps... But then the inlet to the miner would be in that 60c range... So you would definitely want to pass it through a pool or Drycooler before back to the miner to get that approach temp down on the miner HX

**[#683 · 2024-08-19 13:07 · Tyler Stevens]**

**[#685 · 2024-08-19 13:23 · Tyler Stevens]**

There’s a limit on the amount of heat that can transfer. 

The energy balance (heat transfer) is constrained by setting the heat transfer capable to the flow of the water (based on flowrate), equal to the heat transfer capacity of the surface area of hot stuff in the miner.

Ethalpy (internal energy of the water):
Q (heat transfer) = m_water (mass flowrate of water) * Cp (specific heat capacity of water aka thermal inertia) * ∆T (water temp increase).

Energy heat transfer from chip (convection limit):
Q (convection heat transfer from chip to water) = h (convection coefficient based on flow speed and turbulence) * A (surface area of hot chip stuff) * ∆T(chip temp drop)

So:
Q=m_water*cp_water*∆T_water
Q=h_water*A_chip*∆T_chip

therefore:
m*cp*∆T_water = h*A*∆T_chip

Limited by both mass flow of water and chip temp, the only variables in the equation that we can easily tune. But at max wattage, the chip temp is fixed. So water flow is only option.

❤1
👏1

**[#713 · 2024-08-21 21:14 · Bob]**

Yup...definitely an option.  For a home setup, the easiest/cheapest way to start is a single-speed circulator.  Next up is multi-speed manual - small price change and you can change the speed, but its all manual.   Next step up are home-sized ECM motor pumps...where you can start changing pump speed based on temp input.  Cost there is quite a bit more, but now you have some real flexibility in operation.  Then you have industrial stuff with any option you can think of...lots of $$ there, but you're running at scale so it makes sense.   The nice thing is all of this has been figured out already...we just have to apply known technology which is an 'easy' problem to solve 😊

💯2
🔥1

**[#767 · 2024-08-23 10:54 · Tyler Stevens]**

My chapter 4 of the book is all about this.

Comparing three scales of hashrate heating
1. residential and small business
2. industrial operations 
3. Mega miners and pub Co's

Harder to reuse heat at mega miner scale because they are usually rural and dont want the heat themselves.

**[#768 · 2024-08-23 12:25 · Harrison The Space]**

When I was doing bitheat we looked at all of those potential use cases but outside of pool heating and water heating nothing has the utilization rate needed to pay extra expense. If you think of it a Bitcoiner miner is just really expensive resistive heating element. Then when you add additional expense to make that heat in a way that is able to be utilized it’s very hard to justify low utilization rate use cases in terms of ROI

Re microbreweries I think I did the math and even a few thousand watts of continuous heat power would be enough for a huge amount of beer that would out strip their distribution. Most microbrews aren’t continuous processes so you’d need more sqft to have a buffer tank.

**[#769 · 2024-08-23 12:35 · Harrison The Space]**

One issue with industrials low grade heat is cheap. If you have low grade steam that is below 150C it’s mostly useless for many processes or making power off of usually this is put through an economizer to preheat inputs. In terms of plant integration, cost, and complexity it’s a hard sell. That being said specific niches where excess heat isn’t available and smaller operations are probably best chance to make it make sense to operators. In Iowa the ethanol built their own coal plant on site for cogeneration where they reuse every grade of steam. Crazy 2mi long insulated steam conduit.

🔥1

**[#770 · 2024-08-23 13:06 · Cade]**

**[#945 · 2024-09-04 10:43 · Cody Harris]**

Ocean for instance uses a system(Tides) where when you're miners are offline and they find a block you are still paid for the time that you were online (unlike what Braiins used to have)

**[#946 · 2024-09-04 11:04 · Karl]**

As someone who has very intermittent hash rate, fpps is still the best payment structure, especially when there is a fee storm. 

While I am always building shares pointing to ocean, those shares are continuing to get diluted, with fpps I always get the full value for my hash when I'm hashing.

**[#947 · 2024-09-04 11:11 · Cody Harris]**

Not exactly, ultimately Tides ends up working the quite similar to FPPS except for the fact that hashers only get paid out when the pool finds blocks.  Ultimately you are paid out your percentage of the amount of work it took to find the block.  Seems like the most fair system to me

There are ongoing debates about how much miners make on FPPS pools vs Ocean, but the ocean truthers claim they get paid more.

❤1

**[#948 · 2024-09-04 11:12 · Jonathan Y]**

I forget the exact terminology offhand, but it used to be that if you were mining at the time a block hit, you got the reward of the hash at that moment.  So, if you had 100% machines online, you got a 100% value.  But, if you took 50% offline a mere hour before, and it hit, then you would get only 50%.  Worse, if you had only one, and happened to have it offline, and a block hit, you get nothing.  

BUT, I want to say it is FPPS where you get paid based on your total input, not exactly your input when a block hits

**[#949 · 2024-09-04 11:12 · Jonathan Y]**

Braiins used to be that way, but they have now moved over.

👍1

**[#951 · 2024-09-04 11:20 · Jonathan Y]**

I'm not sure exactly what they use, I just mean they moved from "when a block hits" into something different that is weighted for how much work you put in regardless if you are online exactly when a block hits

**[#952 · 2024-09-04 11:21 · Brett Rowan]**

Yes, PPLNS is paid per last n shares, so you get paid based on the esitmated hashrate at the time a block is hit calculated from the last n shares.  

TIDES is different to my understanding - https://ocean.xyz/docs/tides

And then FPPS, if you want to read the spec - https://academy.braiins.com/en/braiins-pool/rewards-and-payouts/#fpps-specification

👍3

**[#953 · 2024-09-04 11:42 · Cody Harris]**

I think Tides is pretty much PPLNS, but the shitty version that Braiins was running tarnished that name to some extent.  So Ocean uses "Tides" distance themselves from PPLNS.  

The difference between Braiins PPLNS and Tides is that the "n shares" in Tides is gigantic so smooths out variance much more than Braiins did.

**[#954 · 2024-09-04 12:31 · Karl]**

The biggest issue is, your shares are getting diluted the longer it takes to find a block. That means if you are switching on an off you aren't accruing shares to make up for the dilution from everyone else running 24/7. So a share earned is worth less and less the longer it takes to find block. With fpps the share is worth exactly what it is at the time. This is especially important during times of high fee volatility. With fpps I get the value from those fees but with a pplns I will miss out if there are no blocks found during the storm.

👍1

**[#955 · 2024-09-04 12:35 · Cody Harris]**

I agree that FPPS is better in times of high fee volatility.  Poor Ocean had a horrible dry spell during the halving fee bonanza😬

The only issue is that there is no way to verify how much of those fees are actually making their way back to the hashers with FPPS.  So some claim that the large FPPS pools are skimming fees and not distributing them as they should

I asked why not link them together. So that the temp monitoring can scale back all the way to 0 hashrate and only leave the control board on.

He thought it was a good idea. Said to add a feature request on the website which creates a formal ticket.

❤1

**[#1079 · 2024-09-07 10:36 · Zack Bomsta]**

You can actually already tune way down in wattage with LuxOS. You do it by creating a bunch of custom profiles and then enabling ATM. The biggest downside of doing this is that your efficiency tanks exponentially the farther you go below 700-800W.

🔥1

**[#1080 · 2024-09-07 10:40 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

I've noticed that too.  I stop at 1200w.  Under 1200w on an s19 is not really worth it.

**[#1081 · 2024-09-07 10:44 · Zack Bomsta]**

I used to stop at 1200W, but Urlacher and the folks at Lux seem to have found the right voltage/hash frequency or something that allows the efficiency to extend a little farther to lower wattages.

❤1

**[#1082 · 2024-09-07 10:50 · Zack Bomsta]**

Honestly though, I think it’s better to run at higher wattage with better efficiencies and just have the miner cycle on and off than it is to run at lower wattage and worse efficiency. So say you only need 600W. I think it’s better to run at 1200W half the time assuming that the thermal capacity of the medium you’re heating is large enough to allow the on/off cycle time to be sufficiently long (>20 minutes). If the cycle time is too short, you’ll spend all your time initializing the miner. It’s definitely application specific, and it’s always a balance.

👍1

4. Management Software. Foreman?

**[#1116 · 2024-09-13 15:30 · Jonathan Y]**

Let's see if I understand this:
Firmware - doing the actual work on the asic and requested by management software or user
API - Automated interface back to the ASIC to allow full control and data gathering
Data Gather - Collection software such as PRTG or management software
3rd party - Interacts with management software OR asic directly via API.  Ex:  Thermostat stating temp is enough, and power down ASIC

**[#1123 · 2024-09-13 15:57 · R D]**

Jonathans explanation covers pretty much everything.  The only thing I'd add is the other direction, the simpler route.   Meaning you can do heat reuse without any of 1-5 really.  An all analog system works and removes complexity.  Next level up would be using #(firmware) but most systems could really stop there.  With the * that it depends on the usecase/rates.  But my system ran for months on stock firmware + analog controls.  Then I used braiins + analog control, then braiins + homeassistant for more granular smart control.  Increased flexibility with increased complexity

👍1

**[#1312 · 2024-10-08 09:45 · Jim ⚡️]**

Hello everyone,
I'm really happy to be here, and I want to thank Tyler for the invite!

My journey as a Heatpunk began in late 2022 when I started experimenting with an S9 miner and BraiinsOS+. People quickly became interested in the concept, especially since, before that, almost no one in France had heard of it. That gave me the chance to name my project, making it easier to talk about and more recognizable. I called it Attakaï, which means "The ideal temperature" in Japanese, a nod to Satoshi Nakamoto.

I also wrote a tutorial, which I published on the PlanB.network (http://PlanB.network) website. I manage a Telegram group where French people can ask questions about building and maintaining their own Attakaï systems. With PlanB Network, we even sell a DIY kit because miners are expensive in France, a plug-and-play version would cost too much, so we focused on making it accessible and educational.

Right now, I’m working on a new design with a single S19 board, an S9 power supply, and 8 fans running on LuxorOS. I'm really happy with the performance and noise levels so far. I've also developed a script that lets users control fan speed and the power profiles is automatically selected using an iOS Shortcut.(work with android too it is just a bash command to sent)

My goal is to make everything as plug-and-play and user-friendly as possible.

❤3
🔥1
🫡1

**[#1335 · 2024-10-08 11:38 · Zack Bomsta]**

One thing to note on the K1Max.: Anyone that does precision machining will tell you that getting a perfectly planar bed is hard, and it gets exponentially harder as the area increases. Now, do it with a thin, economical piece of aluminum (all that the price point of the K1Max can afford). The result: the K1Max print bed can be very nonplanar and unlevel. It’s kind of luck if the draw if you get a good print bed or not. You can buy cheap and effective spring loaded leveler kits on Amazon easy enough to deal with the unlevel issue, but there’s no cheap, easy way to deal with the nonplanar issue. I had to buy an ultra planar 3rd party upgrade kit that was half the price of the printer for 2 of the 5 K1Max printers I bought.

🔥1
💯1

Not sure. I haven’t done much with HA because it makes the system more complex and challenging to deploy at scale in normie homes. What I’ve found is that dynamic power scaling on BOS doesn’t work when targeting low wattage. Its step size is too big, and its adjustment bandwidth is too slow. The result is that the miner spends most of its time overheated and restarting.

**[#1432 · 2024-10-08 19:39 · Dane O]**

I got one installed in Alaska (Juneau so don't know how that rates on your cold scale...) 😉 however most radiant floor systems are rated for around a 50-60c inlet (to the floor system) and a 40c ish return temp which is perfect for the immersion systems operating temps... Also a lot of systems can run lower temps than that (about 95f inlets) and snow melt systems just need the ground just above freezing... 

It's really just based on the size of the system you're using and the BTUs required... A lot of the stuff we're doing is no different than heating with an electric coil or gas furnace just the way you're getting the hot liquid nets you some Bitcoin instead of just burning gas or electricity for heat :)

**[#1520 · 2024-10-12 15:30 · Bob]**

From the photos, it almost looks like it wasn't the contactor itself failing but instead the copper wire - to - contactor interface.  Tough to say since I'm not there and can't see the details, but it might be that the way the wire was pinched down some of the copper strands were bearing more of the current than others and acted as a slow-burn fuse.

If so, a fix is to get some high current crimps.  Crimp the copper into them so you have good wire-crimp connection (you might think about adding some solder if you have a torch or solder gun) and then use the spade interface on the contactor.  If you're using 10 gauge you can use the yellow crimps you can find at any hardware store...they're really cheap. 

https://www.homedepot.com/pep/Gardner-Bender-12-10-AWG-0-25-in-Tab-Female-Fully-Insulated-Disconnect-Yellow-10-Pack-15-155F/205846616?source=shoppingads&locale=en-US&pla&mtc=SHOPPING-CM-CML-GGL-D27E-027_011_TOOLS_ACC-NA-NA-NA-PMAX-NA-NA-NA-NA-NBR-NA-NA-NEW-NA_2024__WHU24&gad_source=1

❤2
👍1

**[#1573 · 2024-10-15 10:32 · Brett Rowan]**

PDU and PSU are different FYI.  PDU is power distribution, so power to multiple devices, and PSU is power supply, so power for one device (generally).  More specifically in mining, a PSU converts input AC voltage (220V-277V generally) to 12V DC for the control board and hashboards.

👍1

**[#1577 · 2024-10-15 12:30 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

has anyone here played with in-line fans that are not made by AC Infinity?  Looking for a low-cost, yet reliable alternative than can also run off 240v power.

**[#1581 · 2024-10-15 23:28 · Zack Bomsta]**

I have. I’ve tried no fewer than 10 cheap alternatives on Amazon. They all sucked. They were way noisier and much more power hungry (way less efficient) than the ACI fans. Also, some of them have a more abrasive tone/pitch (admittedly that’s subjective). I did find 1 alternative that compared very well on noise profile and efficiency, but it didn’t have a tach signal, and the fan blades had a lot of balancing weights on them (sign of poor build quality?). I wasn’t willing to move forward without a tach signal. The Njord board needs a tach signal back from the fan for safety reasons so that it doesn’t spoof if the fan isn’t spinning.

❤2

**[#1590 · 2024-10-16 10:47 · Ryan Ramminger]**

Hey all! Thanks so much for the add, Tyler.

I wanted to take a moment to introduce myself. My name is Ryan Ramminger, and I'm the founder of CryoByte Labs. We provide direct-to-chip liquid cooling solutions for ASIC miners. I've been mining since 2012, and while heating my home in the winter with S9s, I quickly realized how heat reuse could economically benefit mining. I also saw how those consuming heat could help offset their costs with mining rewards.

I wanted to capture the heat in a more usable way (my wife hated the whine of the fans), so I went down a rabbit hole experimenting with different methods. I lapped chips, tested custom immersion setups, and even boiled water on the back of the S9 chips (wouldn't recommend). Eventually, I found that direct-to-chip cooling was the most cost-effective and efficient way to capture heat while properly cooling the machines.

Since then, it’s been a long journey of prototyping, iterating, and working with manufacturers to create our own custom cold plates for single-phase miners. We just officially launched, and I love connecting with anyone working on heat reuse projects. I really enjoy the community and hope to add value or help with anything I can!

👍3

**[#1683 · 2024-10-23 18:00 · Brett Rowan]**

Shouldn’t even be technically possible.

Imagine you have an OS that takes 0.5 seconds to start up.

You apply power to the miner,
Which powers the fans,
The OS starts up,
The OS sends speed signals to the fans.

This means that for 0.5 seconds, the fans can only run at 100%, meaning that as soon as the FW starts up it will try to adjust the fans, but FW takes longer than that to start.

Hence 100% fans

**[#1684 · 2024-10-23 18:02 · Brett Rowan]**

Also, I hesitate to call whatever that Rosseau stuff is “firmware”.  It’s basically a hack that puts a better interface over stock firmware AFAIU (or it was when I tried it).

**[#1694 · 2024-10-24 05:57 · Jim ⚡️]**

Hey Heatpunk team,

I’ve been thinking about how we can push forward our shared goal of transforming Bitcoin miners into heating systems. As you all know, the current mining software options, Braiins, Luxor and Vnish are great but don’t offer the specific flexibility we need to fully optimize miners for heating.

I propose that we come together and approach one (or more) of these software developers to request a custom version that meets our requirements. We could ask for more customizable options that would make it easier and more convenient to use miners as heaters.

This could be a great opportunity not just for us, but for the wider Bitcoin communities. I’d love to hear your thoughts on this, and whether you think we should go with an open letter, white paper, or something else. Let’s brainstorm together and take this to the next level!
Looking forward to hearing your ideas.

❤3

Hey all sorry been outta pocket but wanted to put .02c in... A lot of these have a specific torque spec to keep an eye on too... **[#1703 · 2024-10-24 11:18 · Ryan Ramminger]**

In that video, we used two 360mm radiators (aluminum), 38mm thick, and attached six stock Antminer fans. We only pushed 160 watts total to the fans on max cooling. Yes, in fact that is a huge benefit of the direct-to-chip system. We are able to capture the heat and spread it over any radiator structure, increasing surface area which allows for slow fan speeds, larger fans, or any combination of the two.

**[#1751 · 2024-10-30 15:20 · Tyler Stevens]**

I know I asked this once before but want to make sure i'm not wrong for the hashrate heating book. Thoughts @b_Rowan (https://t.me/b_Rowan) @schnitzeli (https://t.me/schnitzeli) @CoinHeated (https://t.me/CoinHeated) @zbomzz (https://t.me/zbomzz) or anyone else?

The "layers of code for hashrate heating software control are"

1. Firmware (akin to computer motherboard Bios). Custom firmware lets you tweak device settings. (akin to changing voltages or ram speed in computer bios).

2. API / ssh (allows remote access for settings changes to the miner firmware... aka changing firmware without being on the same network as the device. Requires microcontroller connected to the device right?)

3. PyASIC pickaxe / Foreman (are data gathering and management tools that provide the interface to talk to the miners... are these running on the microcontroller or computer talking to the miners on site? So running on the local network)

4. Hass-Miner (third party application integration and GUI for easy remote control and data monitoring... This talks to the microcontroller or computer on the home network that relays commands to the miners?).

**[#1752 · 2024-10-30 15:22 · Brett Rowan]**

2. API / ssh (allows remote access for settings changes to the miner firmware... aka changing firmware without being on the same network as the device. Requires microcontroller connected to the device right?)

Not entirely correct.  API/SSH/Web API all allow an external program to change settings.  This is what foreman, BTCTools, pyasic, etc uses to both read data and write settings to the miner.

💯1

**[#1753 · 2024-10-30 15:24 · Brett Rowan]**

pyasic / forman pickaxe then provide a higher level abstraction of the miners functionality across miner types, so for an API on any given device, pyasic / pickaxe should have an internal function that would call that.  EG pyasic has fault_light_on, which on braiins runs a gRPC command, on antminer stock sends a conf to /cg-bin/set_conf.cgi, on whatsminer it calls the RPC command led, etc.  It is a union of all the miner APIs.

🔥1

**[#1754 · 2024-10-30 15:25 · Brett Rowan]**

Finally, foreman (as most people would think about it, namely, their cloud application), btctools, hass-miner, etc, all use that simplified functionality layer to provide a user experience.  They are the users way of interacting with the miner.

🔥1

The only thing I'd add is that technically, what we usually think of when we say "Firmware" (i.e. Braiins, Vnish, or LuxOS) is an application running on top of Linux. **[#1794 · 2024-10-30 20:28 · Nicolas Drouin-Audet]**

Yes it does happen even if you use cable glands. The oil creeps inside the insulation. This is a common practice in refineries (explosive environment) to seal the cables with epoxy. This is how I temporarily dealt with this issue. The permanent solution is to have a sealed connection.

hey, really good question.  I started with a small (400,000 sat)  ACINQ channel, but I use Aqua wallet to store my mining revenue.  Aqua uses Boltz so I opened a larger channel with the BOLTZ node and now my lightning transactions from my Core Lightning Node (where I receive my Ocean payouts) to my Aqua wallet cost less.  I would research which node your destination wallet uses and open a channel with that node to limit hops and save sats on transaction fees.

**[#2245 · 2024-11-29 12:16 · Brett Rowan]**

Technically this is wrong, there is a target temperature setting, but it’s independent of the power usage and hashrate, since the temp is mostly fan dependent.  It’s pretty much not feasible to make what I think you’re describing, since for any given temperature there isn’t really a stable tuning for the chips, hence why hashrate and power tuning are used as they are relatively stable.

**[#2255 · 2024-11-29 12:32 · Brett Rowan]**

import asyncio  # asyncio for handling the async part
from pyasic.network import MinerNetwork  # miner network handles the scanning

async def scan_miners():  # define async scan function to allow awaiting
    # create a miner network
    # you can pass in any IP and it will use that in a subnet with a /24 mask (255 IPs).
    network = MinerNetwork.from_subnet("192.168.1.50/24")  # this uses the 192.168.1.0-255 network

    # scan for miners asynchronously
    # this will return the correct type of miners if they are supported with all functionality.
    miners = await network.scan()
    print(miners)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    asyncio.run(scan_miners())  # run the scan asynchronously with asyncio.run()

😱1

**[#2265 · 2024-11-29 14:38 · Brett Rowan]**

AFAIU no.  The issue is with different types of miners.  Imaging you have a Whatsminer an S19 with an epic board.  Whatsminer (and most modern miners) expose something colloquially called the “CGMiner API”, which is an RPC API (json payloads via binary stream).  The epic board exposes a web API, which is a completely different protocol, so you can see how that would cause issues.

**[#2382 · 2024-12-02 11:05 · Cody Harris]**

**[#2383 · 2024-12-02 11:11 · Cody Harris]**

My system is reacting to Boiler Fluid Temp.  So fluid gets too hot the miner will downscale.  But because it takes so long for the miner to turn back on I run the risk of the fluid cooling further and triggering a deeper scaling event (which isn't really needed).  

With shorter startup times I can much more precisely tune my system and avoid triggers tripping on each other.

**[#2386 · 2024-12-02 11:20 · Dev 🇳🇿]**

**[#2665 · 2024-12-09 20:29 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

one thing I've learned: regular immersion fluid and pumps isn't really made for these temps, so I actually run the fans at the bottom of the miner (inlet of the case) so they push the oil through them miner. Otherwise the issue is that the oil is already at 90F in the middle of the miner and doesn't realy cool the upper chips of the miner anymore. With the fans it works muuuch better

**[#2666 · 2024-12-09 20:30 · Karl]**

I'm going to bump up the critical temp from 81 to 90 and see what happens.

**[#2668 · 2024-12-09 20:31 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

yea the asics itself can easily get up there, the problem is actually that all other chips on the board don't like the temperture, especially the i2c communication chips start to glitch at high temps and then the hashboard to controlboard communication breaks down, and unfortunately the controlboard assume a single glitch as a failure and doesn't restart the communication unless you completely restart mining (something I would love to fix, but for that we need to build our own control boards)

**[#2669 · 2024-12-09 20:32 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

I actually power them with 12V directly and remove the RPM cable, as in the oil they run much slower anyway and don't make any sounds

**[#2677 · 2024-12-09 20:43 · Michael Schmid @Schnitzel]**

Yes. ESPHome is a firmware for ESP32. ESPHome is specifically built to connect to a HomeAsstant server (you can have as many esphome as you want with a single homeassistan server). This homeassistant is the central brain and can also talk to the miner api through "hass-miner", it can again talk to as many miners as you want.
And yes of course you could just use the homeassistant only and talk to the miners, but it depends on your usecase. If you want to have a temp sensor, actuators (like valves), etc. you need an esphome as the homeassistant itself can't directly talk to a temp sensor. But if you just want to turn on and off miners based on the time then you don't need an esphome.
directly talking from the esphome to the miner is technically possible, but we're using pyasic (python library) to talk to the miners and python doesn't run on esp32, so yea while technically possible it would be a LOT of code

❤1

**[#3022 · 2025-01-02 10:03 · Brett Rowan]**

📢 New Features:
- Introduced a Maximum speed limit configuration option for the fan in auto mode
- Added a minimum preset option for the autoswitcher
- New widget for monitoring water inlet/outlet temperatures on Antminer S19 Pro Hydro and S19 Pro Plus Hydro models
- Expanded support to include submodels HHB68502 and HHB68503 of the Antminer S21 Hydro
- The dashboard now detects and sets the language based on the browser/system settings at the initial start

🚦 Improvements:
- Resolved issues related to User and DevFee pool configurations
- Fixed dashboard freezes caused by error handling
- Enhanced power supply compatibility across different miner models
- Optimized sensor operations on Antminer S21 Pro, S21 XP-Immersion, S21 XP, and S21 Hydro
- System now requires a full reboot when changing the TimeZone
- Added new measures to prevent miner password brute-force attacks
- Strengthened firmware security protocols
- Improved accuracy of miner power consumption graphs
- Enhanced overall miner stability
- Implemented various UI/UX enhancements

🐞 Bug Fixes:
- Corrected network test failures upon network setting adjustments
- Fixed issues with cooling mode resets
- Resolved incorrect hashrate displays on chips when chip frequencies are reduced
- Addressed the issue of the dashboard unlocking multiple times

❗️We are committed to maintaining trust and transparency with our community. As a gesture of our commitment and to make amends for the recent service outage, starting Monday, January 6, 2025, we will temporarily reduce our development fee to the lowest possible rate. This reduction will last for several days and is intended to not only compensate for the lost revenue but also to offer additional relief for the inconvenience caused.

**[#3150 · 2025-01-08 08:20 · Patrick Patel]**

Alright we talked it over and we agreed we’re ready to share what we’re working on. 

So here’s what we built: it’s an electric furnace replacement for central heating powered by 4 Whatsminers (for now).  The design also allows for commercial/industrial space heating and comes in at 14kW. It takes advantage of the existing electrical and ducting infrastructure of any home or business making mining accessible to even those that don’t understand it, but understand saving money on their electrical bill. Best part is, it’s quiet. We use the same style of blower that your typical electric furnace does so the noise is what people already expect.

🔥9

**[#3152 · 2025-01-08 08:20 · Patrick Patel]**

We built a crude POC in my mom’s pottery studio and ran it for about a year (pictured in my profile picture on telegram). From the lessons learned about common issues, we decided to build a control unit to manage the system. Software will be open source when finished, but it essentially uses pyasic to monitor the miners and restart them if they throw errors, as well as restart your router if the internet goes out (this part requires some hardware as well).

After the POC we built our first iteration of a nearly retail ready version (pictured) and have since begun manufacturing of our 2nd iteration.

**[#3167 · 2025-01-08 09:25 · Patrick Patel]**

Operates on the same principle as an electric resistant heater - either it is on or it is off.

When setting it up, whatsminers have a low, normal or high power mode that you can use to adjust output based on the size of the space you are heating. To limit power cycles and increase total hashrate, I tend to prefer more miners on low power mode.

I saw someone mention working on miner firmware/software specifically for heating, and I 100% agree it is a necessity to bring it from a niche industry to people using it in everyday applications without even knowing it. That’s why we had to build the control unit because what average person wants to troubleshoot their heater’s network issues haha

**[#3247 · 2025-01-11 20:06 · Tyler Stevens]**

Yea it's called absorption chilling. Super cool thermodynamic cycle. Essentially an air conditioner powered by heat instead of electricity. Needs higher outlet temps from the ASIC to do this. And I have never seen one that's small scale. But would be epic to have hashrate powered cooling loads.

In the field for off-grid mining, you can pull heat off the genset and the miners to run an organic rankine cycle (heat powered turbine / generator) to get some free power back. Effectively "turbocharging" for bitcoin miners. Get free juice out of the "exhaust"

**[#3287 · 2025-01-12 15:31 · Karl]**

Here is the information I have on my clothes dryer.

What learned, about the heating element of this electric dryer is that it doesn't run continuously throughout the cycle. It powers on gets really hot then powers off. So I'm not sure how well the heat from the miner will actually supplement the heating element. 

Also, much more airflow is necessary than what a dryer moves so it creates positive pressure in the drum with my setup. Pushes lint out into the room.

❤1

**[#3288 · 2025-01-12 15:34 · Patrick Patel]**

I’m assuming the damper will run on 120V, while the heating element in the dryer will run on 240V (two legs of 120V). One leg of the 120V will be gated by a switch, while the other connects directly to the heating element.

To run the damper on 120V only when the heating element is turned on, you will need a common line and to connect to the leg of 120V that is gated by a switch, after the switch. This takes care of the automation of the damper, without needing to buy a separate switch and deal with wiring it.

Something to note:
You will want to make sure sapping power off this 120V line does not put you over the 80% threshold the circuit is designed for

**[#3289 · 2025-01-12 15:39 · Patrick Patel]**

Interesting! So basically:

1) the airflow needed to keep the miner cool enough is too high for what a typical dryer is designed for 

2) the heat cycles are high intensity and short.

Thoughts:
1) sounds like the exhaust hose needs to be sized up

2) seems it might make more sense to replace the heating elements entirely with mining heat

🤔1

**[#3380 · 2025-01-14 14:14 · Ryan Ramminger]**

When you factor in the inline fan power, are your miners typically running at higher, lower, or equal power levels compared to a stock, air-cooled Antminer?

**[#3381 · 2025-01-14 14:25 · Patrick Patel]**

That’s definitely something I need to test, but sadly no, I do not. 

As far as the boards/chips go though, there is not a standard amount of turbulence across the chips - turbulence is dependent on the speed and temperature of the air across the boards. As you increase speed, there will be greater turbulence, creating higher static pressure. This is why I say the fan is more of a concern with higher cfm. 

The chips are solid and I don’t think the static pressure we produce with a fan or blower is high enough to damage them.

👍1
💯1

**[#3382 · 2025-01-14 14:26 · Toine Heat Reuse]**

**[#3558 · 2025-01-16 12:27 · Cade]**

You simply make a boiler loop with a brazed plate HX in the loop. The dry cooler goes outside as a heat dump with the fan on a thermostatic control to keep the fluid consistent up to 57c or so.

When you need to heat the water, turn on the recirculation pump which runs through the HX.

**[#3581 · 2025-01-16 12:39 · Cade]**

Mining user types:

1) Those that need heat and electrical is the most viable type. Power cost is high relative to BTC value. They dont want to run 24/7, they just need heat. Computer type isnt really relevant, within reason. Turn on/off when heat is needed (most of USA)
2) Luxury heat users. Hot tubs, snow melt, etc. where the luxury is worth a value determined by the customer. Vary energy use based on demand of the customer
3) Straight up mining. Runs 24/7 to maximize profits. Low power cost relative to production value. 
4) Idealogues. Power cost doesnt matter.

❤4
👍1
💯1

python - 

# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

#  Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, software         -
#  distributed under the License is distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS,           -
#  WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied.    -
#  See the License for the specific language governing permissions and         -
#  limitations under the License.                                              -
# ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

import asyncio

class MinerListenerProtocol(asyncio.Protocol):
    def __init__(self):
        self.responses = {}
        self.transport = None
        self.new_miner = None

    async def get_new_miner(self):
        try:
            while self.new_miner is None:
                await asyncio.sleep(0)
            return self.new_miner
        finally:
            self.new_miner = None

    def connection_made(self, transport):
        self.transport = transport

    def datagram_received(self, data, _addr):
        if data == b"OK\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00\x00":
            return
        m = data.decode()
        if "," in m:
            ip, mac = m.split(",")
            if "/" in ip:
                ip = ip.replace("[", "").split("/")[0]
        else:
            d = m[:-1].split("MAC")
            ip = d[0][3:]
            mac = d[1][1:]

        self.new_miner = {"IP": ip, "MAC": mac.upper()}

    def connection_lost(self, _):
        pass

class MinerListener:
    def __init__(self, bind_addr: str = "0.0.0.0"):
        self.found_miners = []
        self.stop = asyncio.Event()
        self.bind_addr = bind_addr

    async def listen(self):
        loop = asyncio.get_running_loop()

        transport_14235, protocol_14235 = await loop.create_datagram_endpoint(
            MinerListenerProtocol, local_addr=(self.bind_addr, 14235)
        )
        transport_8888, protocol_8888 = await loop.create_datagram_endpoint(
            MinerListenerProtocol, local_addr=(self.bind_addr, 8888)
        )
        try:
            while not self.stop.is_set():
                tasks = [
                    asyncio.create_task(protocol_14235.get_new_miner()),
                    asyncio.create_task(protocol_8888.get_new_miner()),
                ]
                await asyncio.wait(tasks, return_when=asyncio.FIRST_COMPLETED)
                for t in tasks:
                    if t.done():
                        yield t.result()

        finally:
            transport_14235.close()
            transport_8888.close()

    async def cancel(self):
        self.stop = True

if __name__ == "__main__":

    async def main():
        async for m in MinerListener().listen():
            print(m)

    asyncio.run(main())

The C1 kit is great for water heat because it has a heat dump outside. 

Fluid flow is Tank>plate HX>external dry cooler>Tank

**[#3913 · 2025-02-09 06:44 · Jason]**

So, I’d be under that 80% mark to use a single 40 amp breaker for two machines, no? Google tells me this is a bad idea, but I’m coming up with less than 27 amps, which looks like it’s within the limits unless I’m way worse at simple division than I thought. Haha

👍1

**[#3914 · 2025-02-09 06:51 · R D]**

3200w x2 = 6400w
6400w / 240v = 26.6A ~27

240x40 = 9600w
X.8 = 7680w

Yep your math is right and that romex works. 

However if you want to run custom firmware with modified power limits for over clocking you are limiting your head room sharing a circuit. I did 2 30 amp circuits instead of one bigger one. You could do a 60 amp but the wire gets quite stiff and a pain to work with. Up to you. Not sure how long your run is. But yes your math is correct

👍1

**[#3915 · 2025-02-09 06:53 · R D]**

If you're over clocking it's standard to push that 3200w to like 4000w. You could push slightly more but usually the next bottleneck is the 20amp c19 cables

I use 50A for 2 miners exactly for this reason (overclock). I suggest you do the same. 60A for 3 miners

**[#3964 · 2025-02-12 08:17 · Jake⚡️]**

One of the arguments against heating with bitcoin mining I get is the air to air / air to water pumps and their SCOP values being like 4-5 whatever. I have been heating my guesthouse for 3 years during Winther months and as most of you here we do it for ideological and educational reasons etc. but I want to ask how you would argue against these alternatives with COP values >1 and the sats generated?

**[#3965 · 2025-02-12 08:38 · Cody Harris]**

Oh I had to look up SCOP, it's similar to the SEER ratings in the US.

The problem with this comparison is that it assumes the "price" of energy is fixed, but when hash heating you are essentially reducing what was assumed to be fixed. 

Heat pumps use less energy for sure(to a point) there is no argument there.  The only good argument is that yes Hash heating probably uses more energy ie lower SCOP value, but it costs costs less to operate. 

Heat pumps are electrically efficient.
Hash Heat is financially efficient.

❤1

**[#3966 · 2025-02-12 08:40 · Cody Harris]**

**[#3968 · 2025-02-12 08:49 · Nicolas Drouin-Audet]**

The 4-5 COP is typically at 15C, when you don't need that much heat. COP drop to 2-3 range when outside temp is -5, and even lower for lower temps. My take on this is you have to look at the total cost of ownership over a few years. This is where Bitcoin plays an important role. Here's a calculator I built for my customers to help them evaluate the payback of my heaters compared to other heating source. It all depends on the electricity price, Bitcoin price appreciation and hashrate price index.

**[#3984 · 2025-02-12 09:16 · Karl]**

If participation in the Bitcoin network has any value to you, then you could argue that using the miners for heat are doing two functions vs a heating appliance that only does heat. Not to mention the Bitcoin "rebate". 

By using the miner for two things, heat and mining, then it is hands down more efficient than any other energy expenditure being used for heat alone. 

Similarly, using the heat off the engine of a car to heat the interior would be using the fuel for two things at once.

If the environment is the concern, the concerned individual should reduce waste and increase yeilds per unit of energy and time spent to have the greatest impact. 

Naturally, this action also puts a limit on the scale of the individuals production before waste is created. Often this waste is hidden by externalizing the direct consequence of the waste.

**[#4123 · 2025-02-18 21:31 · Dev 🇳🇿]**

Hi guys, I’m going to go see an accountant on Monday to learn about the accounting requirements for selling heat via bitcoin mining. 

I’d appreciate any advice regarding specific questions to ask or areas to get more clarity on. I appreciate accounting works differently worldwide but for the most part I’m sure there’s a lot of commonalities. 

Let me know!

**[#4126 · 2025-02-19 10:06 · R D]**

It's not much different than any other business.  It's all inputs andd outputs.  I keep track of the $ amount of electricity used per month. And I keep track of the $ value of the sats as they hit the wallet. (No exact sat amounts, don't want gov to track utxos and they only care about dollars anyway)

**[#4127 · 2025-02-19 10:11 · Patrick Patel]**

Depends what country you’re in, but RD is right, just inputs and outputs, income and expenses. I’m not an accountant, but this has been our experience

Expenses - electricity, equipment (although treated slightly differently due to depreciation), etc.
Income - usd value of mined bitcoin at time of mining, usd income from heat sold. 

Essentially you just subtract expenses from income and you have your taxable profit. If you’re interested, my father in law is a CPA and has been handling our business taxes if you want his contact

👍1

**[#4203 · 2025-02-23 12:31 · Bob]**

Most of these machines are made using standard electronics fabrication techniques/methods.   The industry has largely gotten rid of the really nasty stuff.  You'll get some volatile chemicals coming of the hardware when you first run them, but that's the same as any other product with a PCB board in it.   Another  thought—most of these boxes carry a CE mark.   That pretty much means you meet all 'materials of concern' issues...the euro union and California are the two most difficult markets for MOCs.   If you have a CE mark, you probably can eat off of it, LOL

❤3
👍1

**[#4206 · 2025-02-23 12:37 · Bob]**

**[#4430 · 2025-03-06 16:07 · Travis Bitkle]**

What is the voltage per leg on your three phase? Note that the power cord has four wires. I'm assuming that is three hot legs and a ground. Per the Bitmain specs, they want 380V-415v 3 PH. That comes out to 220v-240v per leg. In my case, I'm at about 250v per leg. A little hot, but should work fine.

**[#4431 · 2025-03-06 16:08 · Travis Bitkle]**

So I think delta will work in this case because of the 4 wire power cord. But verify your voltage per leg.

**[#4562 · 2025-03-18 23:34 · Dane O]**

Convection? If hot oil was flowing through the BPHX the whole time it would be heating up the water inside and as the hot water rises and the cooler sinks inside your HX it might be "leaking" out to where your temperature sensors are located as a thermal "bleed over" (technical term I know 😜) so if this spike is a problem aka logic running off these temperatures maybe try moving the sensor a litter further down the line... And you probably won't get those reading anymore until the pump is on...

**[#4641 · 2025-03-20 15:35 · Jonathan Y]**

Fun fact:  The two tanks were the very first two immersion tanks I setup when I got into mining.  They were full of miners until I realized it was not an optimal solution.  But then I just had these big tanks leftover doing nothing.  And I didn't want two separate tanks.  And since they were already mine anyway, I just found a 7" diamond blade hole saw, rigged up some pink foam template, and went to town on drilling through 1" of glass.  The real trick to drilling glass is to go slow, and keep it wet/cool.  Then it's easy.  I think it took about 30 to 50 min or so.

**[#4854 · 2025-03-24 15:04 · Travis Bitkle]**

According to the robot...

Yes, galvanic corrosion (often linked to electrolysis in a system) can occur when dissimilar metals like copper and aluminum are in electrical contact in a conductive fluid. However, glycol solutions—especially when formulated with corrosion inhibitors—are generally less conductive than pure water, which reduces the risk. Furthermore, if you electrically isolate the aluminum and copper components by using segments of plastic tubing as dielectric isolators, you break the electrical pathway that drives galvanic corrosion. This isolation is an effective measure to help prevent electrolysis and the related corrosion issues.

👍1
👀1

**[#4863 · 2025-03-24 20:27 · Dane O]**

Don't want to be Debbie downer but just wanted to make a public service announcement in relation to this...

Engine coolant vs actual hydronic coolant is quite different in additives and chemical composition, but the main one is ethylene vs propylene glycol...

DO NOT ever plan on using a domestic water HX (aka hydronic loop to heat your domestic hot water) with ethylene glycol (engine coolant) as if/when the HX fails it will pump toxic chemicals throughout your domestic/drinking water... 

That is why most systems use propylene glycol as it's "safer" for consumption I guess it's what all the kids are smoking these days anyways (propylene glycol)

Don't want this to sound like a call out, love the reuse over trashing a chemical to go into our groundwater! Just wanted to make note for anyone else planning on this...

Tldr ethylene glycol never exchange with something you will possibly consume (dhw)

Propylene glycol 👍 (ish) 😜

👍5

**[#5118 · 2025-03-29 10:17 · Josh]**

Initial observations/thoughts after running for a few days:
 1. A radiator, heat dump, or using the miner fans is necessary if you want to get the HWH up to 140f in a reasonable time. You can go from 110 -> 130 no problem but after that the differential is too small and boards overheat unless you dump some excess
 2. I’m running a cron job to sleep the miner during peak power times and at night. It turns back on in the morning so it’s ready for showers, laundry etc.. Might need to add more snooze intervals unless you need the heat elsewhere. I like this approach more than a home assistant setup since it is way simpler. Also I was never able to get HA to work with Luxor
 3. Luxor ATM feature scales up and down very nicely as the HWH calls for heat. I set the ATM timing to 1 minute and it adjusts quickly. If someone’s having a long shower it can overclock like a champ as the cold water comes into the tank
 4. Luxor doesn’t let you shut down individual boards via API if you’re using ATM (ytho?). It would be nice to cut down to one board once the HWH temp is getting close to/at target (135f-140f).
 5. We don’t use enough hot water to justify the full 3kw. This unit could easily provide heat to half of my house in addition to the HWH. It could be a “central heat mine” and have branches to radiator in the HVAC, pool, etc..
 6. Pex and pipe insulation is cheap - you don’t need the miner to be next to the HWH if you can run pipes from wherever you have power
 •  7. Need to insulate the components (tubing, HX, reservoir) otherwise the room will get pretty warm (unless you want that)

❤5
👀2

## 2025-04

**[#5246 · 2025-04-01 09:52 · Tyler Stevens]**

HIgher or lower difficulty submissions to a pool will change the frequency of shares you submit but not the reward you receive.

**[#5247 · 2025-04-01 09:52 · Tyler Stevens]**

Changing the frequency of your submissions is good if you have a lot of hashrate versus very little hashrate. By adjusting up or down based on what you are running at your mine, you're able to moderate how often you need to communicate with the pool (so you don't flood them with tons of low difficulty work because you have a huge mine, or almost never ping them because share difficulty is way too high for your tiny mine).

🔥2
💯1

**[#5248 · 2025-04-01 09:53 · Tyler Stevens]**

I thought this was super helpful for those of us with smaller mining for heat ops

**[#5252 · 2025-04-01 13:06 · Brett Rowan]**

Another way to think about this is similar to network diff.  Pool shares are the same as valid blocks but with a lower difficulty.  In the same way that difficulty is adjusted based on how many miners are on the network to keep block time to ~10 minutes, your mine should have a difficulty that will allow it to send shares with a certain frequency, and this has to be adjusted based on how many miners you have.  Pool mining sort of mirrors the network in a cool way :)

💯2

**[#5867 · 2025-04-29 17:54 · Dane O]**

Looking good! Could be the fact that your box is plastic and everything is isolated from the ground, also that oil (Canola) is possibly generating static electricity through the pumping of the fluid, this was a huge issue on early immersion systems using PVC or non metallic pipes not being grounded appropriately as the fluid pumping would generate a huge amount of static... Enough to throw you across the room... Long story short make sure your psu ground (neutral) pin has continuity to your ground... But this also all depends on how your panel is wired feeding the miner (bonded or not)

**[#6024 · 2025-05-17 08:26 · Elijah Sanders]**

$/th/d = $0.0557

Efficiency= j/th

$/th/d ÷ 24h = $

$ ÷ j/th = $/w/h

$/w/h x 1000w (1kw) = $/kWh 

$/kWh break-even

## 2025-06

**[#6334 · 2025-06-01 01:02 · Christian Naef]**

We tested a more or less identical unit, and it is a mining unit, not a heating product. At RY3T, we build heating systems that use Bitcoin mining as a heat source. The Heatcore does not function as a proper heating system. The pipes are too small, and the heat exchanger is far too small. Why would you even need fans?

We built a product that you don’t have to think about. You get a system that simply works. I understand that some people want DIY solutions, and there are products out there for that. But our philosophy is different. People should not need to be deeply involved in mining or the technology behind it. They just need to like Bitcoin.

We take care of everything for them. At the same time, they can still choose their own mining pool and have some flexibility if they want to. We believe that we can decentralize hashrate much more effectively if we offer a product that people do not have to worry about. It works out of the box, comes with a warranty, and is installed by professional plumbers.

Let’s decentralize hashrate and bring Bitcoin to the masses through heating. We also make use of surplus solar energy and do everything we can to maximize miner uptime by using energy as efficiently as possible.

👍3

**[#6336 · 2025-06-01 01:12 · Christian Naef]**

Yes, we partnered with Winchain for the MINI version. We have known each other for two years. This year we decided to merge our expertise. We brought in the heating expertise, and they are the best mining infrastructure engineers we have met.

**[#6408 · 2025-06-07 22:12 · Travis Bitkle]**

The problem I was having was that when the aquastat triggered the valves to open was that a big slug of 115F water would dump back to the miner that has 170F water. This would throw an error code.

👀1

**[#6409 · 2025-06-07 22:13 · Travis Bitkle]**

The other problem was that the zone valves would open too slow, so there was a brief period where the pump would dead head. The exacerbated the temp swing problem because flow would stop, increasing chip temp a bit, then there would be 50F colder water dump in.

👌2

**[#6410 · 2025-06-07 22:18 · Travis Bitkle]**

Now it is set up with a primary and secondary loop. The water heater now pulls from the primary loop hot or supply, and the aquastat triggers the relay for the secondary pump. But here's the trick. The water heater return water dumps right back into the hot line just on the other side of the green relay box. What this does is allow the return water from the water heater to mix through the whole loop out to the radiator, and in the radiator. No temp shock to the miner. When the pump isn't running when there is no call for heat, the primary loop just pumps right on past out to the garage radiator.

❤1

**[#6412 · 2025-06-07 22:19 · Travis Bitkle]**

This setup allows for the miner to run full time but also puts full on hot water to the water heater for fastest recharge when there is a call for heat as well as accommodating the finicky miner.

🔥2

**[#6516 · 2025-06-23 10:11 · Jarno]**

Hello heatpunks, glad to be among you! My heat re-use plan is to install a hydro miner setup at our two-family house which at the moment is heated with geothermal heat pump. The hydro-miner-heat-pump would provide heat via exchangers (not necessarily in this order) first to our domestic hot water loop, second to radiator loop, third it would pre-heat the liquid going into the geothermal pump and fourth the excess heat would go to the geothermal wells (which would conveniently make the cooling basically silent without the need for a dry cooler and in the best case scenario would store some heat in the wells). We got some solar panels installed so that would provide watts for the miner(s) and the spot-price of electricity here in Finland is quite cheap, for now at least. My shortlist is Heatcore's HS20 or HS05 or a local startup's "Bitcoin Boiler". The installation will be quite expensive so any insights are welcome, not going to diy this shit 😅 But I have been researching this for over a year and it should work out, the biggest hurdle is convincing the other residents here as you know.. At the moment I'm mining with a couple of M50S's and S19k's but the heat is wasted. Immersion setup for the current miners is a possibility, but I'm kind of hesitant to start messing with the oil..

**[#6583 · 2025-06-25 09:47 · Dane O]**

Same with the systems I have running/installed... (With 2x a day curtailment periods) The pool hoping reference is why they do the tides and 8 block averages, so if you "hop on and off" or "intermittent on and off" in old pool structures you could get full payment of the block if it was found during your on time...

That's where the tides and the 8 block average comes into play, it's all based on your Hashrate during that timeframe (not full on and off ) you could see it based on the "estimate earnings for next block" box on the ocean dashboard.

Plus the added benefit of miner template construction is a whole other thing to unpack for improving decentralization.

**[#6585 · 2025-06-25 10:02 · Dane O]**

**[#6587 · 2025-06-25 11:55 · Karl]**

With FPPS you get paid the full value at the time, with tides you get paid the diluted value once the block is found. The less uptime you have the less shares you have. No doubt you are getting paid out correctly based but you would earn more on an fpps with intermittent hashrate.

**[#6588 · 2025-06-25 11:57 · Karl]**

This suggests that ocean is missing out on fees too, probably getting unlucky and hitting blocks during times of low fees. With FPPS you get paid based on the fee market at the time you're hashing.

**[#6651 · 2025-06-28 18:23 · Karl]**

You're right, I had most of the components already. The pumps and heat exchanger cost $136. And I had to buy $60 of canola oil (which I could have gotten cheaper if I tried). What I'm trying to exemplify here is utilizing permaculture to build mining systems. 

Slow and small solutions, heat reuse system should start with what you have first then slowly upgrade over time as you get more familiar with the system to arrive at a solution that best fits the use case. I actually started with a heat exchanger that I already had then upgraded it after I built a proof of concept.

Now I'm upgrading the the hashboards from s19 to s19 KPro (which I took the Bitcoin that I mined with the system to pay for the upgrade of both the hashboard and the heat exchanger).

Produce no waste and use and value renewable resources and materials: most people throw things away when they can actually be repurposed into other uses once it is no longer adequate for its original purpose. I tend to save these bits of trash for use in future projects. Additionally since most people throw good stuff away, I consider the flow of trash to be a renewable resource which I can get and repurpose for my projects with very little cost (many times free). Once again, if your building slow and small you have the time to aquire all the components you need from the waste stream at much lower cost than buying new. 

It's very hard to end up with more sats than you started with a mining system even considering building with used equipment and stuff you already have laying around. 

If you're running a business selling systems to people, the are probably buying it because they don't have the bandwidth or intellectual/experiencial capital to diy so they will likely never mind the sats back from the system (minus power cost and considering the value of the heat which I think is worth about 30 percent in my situation). Maybe they just like Bitcoin and they just want to participate. But if you want to truly end up with more Bitcoin you gotta diy permaculture style.

**[#6818 · 2025-07-24 13:47 · ₿ Minion]**

My fellow telegram subscribers:

A new type of scam is spreading on Telegram: scammers can get your account deleted just by tricking you into pressing one button.

Here’s how it works step by step:

1️⃣ The scammer convinces you to press the Start button on an unfamiliar bot.

2️⃣ They then begin blackmailing you, threatening to get your account banned unless you pay them.

3️⃣ If you ignore them, the scammer renames the bot to something official-looking like Telegram Wallet, Support Bot .

4️⃣ Then they transfer ownership of the bot to you (yes, that’s possible).

5️⃣ Finally, they mass-report the bot, which leads to your account being banned along with the bot.

⚠️ Be cautious: Don’t interact with unknown bots or press Start unless you’re sure the bot is safe.

**[#6842 · 2025-07-25 22:46 · Christian Naef]**

Because water based heating systems are full of dirt. you don‘t want that stuff in your miner. Furthermore, you can control the temperature you reach by controlling pump speed of primary and secondary circuits.

🤔1

**[#6843 · 2025-07-25 22:48 · Christian Naef]**

yes, you can also attache a dry cooler. Through a valve you change the loops.

**[#6845 · 2025-07-26 06:03 · Christian Naef]**

The pipes are not 100% ohygen-proof which can contaminate the water over time. In a water based heating systems with floor heating or radiators, the same water circulates in the pipes for several years. You don't want this dirt in your miner.

**[#7111 · 2025-08-19 06:19 · Jon hashing2heating]**

Ive tried to get different liability insurances and they always could not get if figured out how to quote me as a "consultant and supplier/sales rep of MFG equipment made by others. so it comes back to what I was told about UL by Zack from Loki Boards.. first it usually does not matter about UL until you make 10,000 units.. (I paraphrase and not legal advise). but that it is not actually ever required it is just a thing to have.  (like the better business rating..) 
but since it IS so interwoven to our insurance minded world, the answer was that if the components used to produced the system ARE UL rated  (PSUs, pumps, etc) and the asics are shown to have a shut down temp limit) than there is nothing different from that and a traditional water heater or radiant heat electric boiler )
they do not require Carrier to "insure" every roof-top AC air handler but they DO carry their insurance that covers ALL mechanical systems of the building? so it is more back on them on how is the property and everything on it currently covered. The auxiliary heating system with hashrate (esp if it is stand alone and the existing is left as a back up) should be viewed as an additional "appliance" being installed inside the existing fully insured Built Environment  in question,
at least thats my take on it, DYOR and not legal or final advice. - def want to follow this thread, as it has always been an issue for me too. I did try for a while to get some, and they just didn't get why I would need it (for doing what I do), and to have installing contractors to do the work or a general contractor to pull it together for the facility.

❤5

**[#7113 · 2025-08-19 14:15 · Jon hashing2heating]**

Id also say to check out how heat-bit is approaching it? they seem to be the most main stream of the units out there? (despite my sorrow that they are) When I looked into it at one point they had the wording "designed in accordance with" UL 1042.. but it did NOT say it was UL rated.. they have different levels of ratings as well. so it does get down to creative semantics and how you want to be an LLC or a public CO or get investors that need more coverage ?  if you are offering warranty on the systems being sold, then it probably makes sense to look a UL at some point .

**[#7145 · 2025-08-20 18:49 · Karl]**

It sounds like everything relies on the performance of your node. If you are filtering out sub 1 sat transactions for example, it might take several seconds for your node to verify the latest block because it has to call for those missing transactions from the broader network. Meanwhile you're hashing on a stale template, throwing away work. I wonder if ocean would accept those shares. Instances like that might lead to lower payouts.

**[#7146 · 2025-08-20 18:54 · Dylan Seib]**

Although, I imagine it would be negligibly lower payouts due to the OCEAN share window being 8x the current difficulty worth of shares. Nonetheless, I think OCEAN would still recognize the shares on a 'stale' template like this as their templates filter for <1sat/vB. I'll have to dive back into the docs to research.

Does anyone know if the cryobyte water cooling kits work with jpro+ or is it strictly jpro? Any other s19 that it will work with?

JPro+ has soldered on heat sinks so I'd say no.

👍1

**[#7509 · 2025-11-01 08:32 · Gianluca L]**

I think yes, just you need to remove the original heatsinks and solder paste

**[#7513 · 2025-11-01 12:44 · Gianluca L]**

Even the same models mount different hashboards, or just different heatsinks. In the case of S19k pro, for example, the first series mounts single heatsinks soldered on the Asics, from the second series they mount plate heatsinks with thermal paste. So it depends on what you have in your hand regardless of the miner model. If you have soldered heatsinks, what you can do is disassemble the heatsinks (as if you were replacing a chip) and then absorb and remove the solder paste from the surface. Then you can then mount any other type of heatsink. The model is not decisive, it is a generic procedure

**[#7514 · 2025-11-01 12:46 · Gianluca L]**

What is certain is that to remove the soldered heatsinks and solder paste you need to have the equipment. So the same air pump you need to replace a chip.

**[#7519 · 2025-11-01 14:16 · Gianluca L]**

I can't see kpro kit in the cryobyte website, but, what I think is that the size of the hashboards, the position and the number of Asics is different from one model to another. In addition, these kits are designed to be installed inside the original cases, which also have slightly different dimensions.

**[#7650 · 2025-11-13 13:43 · Nicolas Drouin-Audet]**

You won’t. My preferred option would be shell & tubes because more resistant to scaling & more robust. However in order to get the exchange area required it has to be quite large, so this is why I’m using plate frame HX for all my systems. Make sure you can backwash the HX if something goes wrong. You can also add an additional strainer, it’s fairly cheap.

**[#7753 · 2025-11-15 09:16 · Tyler Stevens]**

What's the reasoning for going the custom route vs home assistant? Curious if you have found some tradeoffs for either.

**[#7754 · 2025-11-15 09:24 · Tyler Stevens]**

In theory, I'm leaning towards home assistant because it already has the community and active development in the broader IOT ecosystem. 

Meaning we don't have to recreate integrations for various smart thermostats or temperature sensors or whatever. It's already done.

**[#7761 · 2025-11-15 13:33 · Gianluca L]**

At the top, there is the user experience, we aim to make the miner manageable in a completely autonomous, zero-knowledge way. The target is different, in our case it is anyone who is not a technician, even the grandmother. It represents the largest slice in favoring decentralization, so they are not "competitors", so to speak. In addition, conceptually it wants to be a hardware platform that allows you to develop any type of mining hardware application, even where the goal is not domestic heating. Any type of sensor or control can be used with the raspi gpios. The final idea is a multifunctional platform that stands between the user and mining, to make it easier to apply to your project without deep knowledge about miners management. Most of work for us is to build the communications between raspi and new mining things, new models, new firmwares, etc and in the same time we are working to the heating part, trying to create automatic presets to optimize profitability based on the heat required and the lowest possible consumption adjusting chips frequency, which vary over time by scenarious. Everything is ready for an inexperienced user who will only have to set up an app, once the miner and the system are turned on.

**[#8155 · 2025-12-05 15:19 · Tyler Stevens]**

Does anyone have experience running a miner off three phase 208 V power?

**[#8157 · 2025-12-05 15:54 · Tyler Stevens]**

exactly my confusion. Commercial project is getting three phase 208 V service

**[#8158 · 2025-12-05 16:12 · Tyler Stevens]**

@TravisBitckle (https://t.me/TravisBitckle) how are you powering that S19 hydro?

**[#8159 · 2025-12-05 16:27 · Travis Bitkle]**

In my case the building has three phase coming in 120v per leg (hot leg to neutral), each leg 120 degrees off phase with each other. Hot leg to hot leg voltage is 208v. I have a 208 Delta to 480Y277 step up transformer. The three hot leads enter the transformer at 120v, exit at about 250v per leg (I have it tapped down some from 277v). Then the three legs of 250v power the PDU's and the hydro machine.

I think 208v three phase is a bit a misnomer. It is likely 120v per leg, but line to line is 208v.

**[#8161 · 2025-12-05 16:29 · Travis Bitkle]**

Long story short, if you want to power Antminer hydros, you'll need a step up transformer so far as I can tell.

👍1

**[#8162 · 2025-12-05 16:29 · Tyler Stevens]**

Ideally, Auradine AH3880s. Backup plan is M63, worst case, M64 single phase. I want hydro and U form factor standards for future proofing

**[#8163 · 2025-12-05 16:32 · Travis Bitkle]**

The standard I believe is going to be between 240v and 277v per leg. The single phase machines can run on 277, but technically not rated for above 250v. In my case I wanted to cheat the voltage up as much as I could get away with to allow for more amps per outlet on the PDU's. No load I'm at like 260v per leg, under full on load it runs around 245v-250v.

@tylerkstevens (https://t.me/tylerkstevens) (Tyler Stevens) posted in Hashrate Heating vs Heat Pumps - Calculate COP with BTC (https://heatpunks.org/t/hashrate-heating-vs-heat-pumps-calculate-cop-with-btc/233/1)

Some fudsters say hashrate heating makes no sense when heat pumps exist. 
Here’s why I disagree 
Heat pumps don’t generate heat. They move heat. Say you spend 1 unit of electricity on a heat pump. You could get 3 units of heat energy in return. How? Because they capture heat from the ambient air and bring it inside. Even when it’s cold outside, there’s technically still some energy to extract from...

**[#8391 · 2025-12-22 21:01 · Jonathan Y]**

Hello all.  Been a good long while since I've been around.  Lots of life changes that are finally starting to settle down.  I've still been heating my house with asics, but just put into the air vent.  Alas, it is finally time I start to pick up my long-term goal of heating my fish tank with asics.  Took me a year just to get the tank up with water in it, another year with goldfish, killed all those off, and two years with koi, all unheated.  Just gave the koi away today, and now finally moving to tropical fish, so, it's time to actually look into asic water heating of a fish tank for real.  

I'm thinking the most basic setup would be to do a single S19/S21 card with a hydro block swapped out onto it.  The tank is a 250 gallon main tank with about 40g sump (actual water).  Target temp is 85F, so it will need non-stop heat.

But, since I've been out for so long, maybe there is something new out there?  Something easier to manage for water heating specifically?

**[#8392 · 2025-12-22 21:05 · Karl]**

Is your plan to test heating the tank without fish in it? How many watts does a off the shelf tank heater require for 250gal? I can't imagine it's very much.

**[#8393 · 2025-12-22 21:07 · Jonathan Y]**

I would test with fish in it.  I would set it up with a thermostat controlled power that would cut power if it got above 87F.  As of this moment, I'm using an 800w unit and it is very slowly heating it up.  I suspect it would manage to keep up OK, which is why I was considering a single card, since one card could get as low as 550w or so.  Longer term goal would be to have it flip between idle and hash within firmware instead of hard on/off at power

**[#8395 · 2025-12-22 21:13 · Jonathan Y]**

For sure one would be enough, since even one board can go up to 1500w.  My bigger question is how.  The most basic setup I could think of would be to do an aftermarket waterblock, but not sure that's ideal.  I could build an immersion tank, and just put the whole thing in the sump and transfer heat that way.  But I had some aluminum in the water before and it seemed to build up stuff on it, so not sure if that's great.  Could do stainless steel perhaps though, but I've have to buy that, and it is MUCH harder to work with.

**[#8396 · 2025-12-22 21:16 · Karl]**

You could put the miner in a cooler filled with oil then run a coil through it with water from the tank. That would allow for a single loop and be pretty cheap/simple to try. The biggest risk there is leaking water into the oil tank.

**[#8397 · 2025-12-22 21:18 · Jonathan Y]**

Yes, I've done a similar setup before when I heated my swimming pool  I did also look into the stainless "cooling wort" that are stainless.  Could do a few of those in the sump perhaps.  But not a great form factor.  I'd go back to the "water to air heat exchangers" I used in the past, but I don't think they will hold up well to the tank water.  That leaves me back in the stainless or titanium heat exchanger realm of things

**[#8538 · 2026-01-04 06:57 · Gianluca L]**

We used itead sonoff systems for years for power monitoring, we built entire mining farms with it. To manage data you must use a (your) central server. Basically you need a database to store the data you get from power sensors. MySQL work but better is influxDB, because allow you to build on/off controls on a monitor system like graphana. Important is the protocol: we used mqtt to get data every 30s from sensors by wifi

Hailed heat pumps of Germany's energy transition, especially monoblock models, often use flammable refrigerants such as propane. During prolonged power outages, safety mechanisms (e.g., pumps or valves) fail, which can lead to gas accumulation or leaks. Ignition sources pose a risk of explosion. short answer: yes
long answer: they're a great place to start your home automation journey, but most require third party servers. there are some (like ESPHome) that let you have total local control without a third party though, and those are great. Different trade offs for different protocols. Zigbee, Zwave, Matter, Thread, Wifi all solve slightly different problems.

**[#9804 · 2026-04-08 19:00 · Karl]**

I'm experimenting with blending mineral oil and canola oil to lower the viscosity and increase longevity

**[#9805 · 2026-04-08 19:01 · Karl]**

Canola oil is much cheaper than mineral oil so it will lower the overall cost of immersion setups

**[#9806 · 2026-04-08 19:01 · Trevor Bello]**

How well was that canola oil working? How long of a life cycle were you getting from it and do you think it negatively impacted the lump and or miners?

**[#9807 · 2026-04-08 19:02 · Trevor Bello]**

Im thinking about setting up my immersion stuff but don't really want to buy any oil so I've been contemplating buying canola oil lol

**[#9808 · 2026-04-08 19:05 · Karl]**

The canola oil lasts about 8 months with vitamin e oil the way I'm using it but I think it can last longer with less exposure to oxygen/aeration and lower max temperature. 

I tried an experiment without the vitamin e oil and it lasted 6 months. 

Now I'm trying to cut the thick oil with mineral oil to see what happens. So far it's improving the performance but I haven't found the sweet spot on the ratio yet. Right now I'm at 6:1 canola : mineral oil

**[#9809 · 2026-04-08 20:41 · Trevor Bello]**

Well I might just fafo. I need to setup my 220v subpanel in my shed before I can power on the tank that i have. So that'll come first then I can just do things lol.

Keep tinkering with that ratio and I'll get back to ya. All o know is that cheap sounds better than efficient lol

**[#9810 · 2026-04-08 21:16 · Karl]**

I'm trying to find uses for the spent oil too. If I can make some sawdust logs with it and heat my house for a day or two or make fire starters to sell, something to get some additional value out of the oil.

So far I haven't noticed any degradation in the boards and I've been able to clean them up and hash air cooled again too.

**[#9811 · 2026-04-08 22:30 · Mike Clear]**

Karl, sawdust logs work if you have sawdust. You can also make “fatwood” All you need to do is soak wood in the oil until it absorbs. Ideally, kindling that's no thicker than one inch. It takes time, but less effort than the sawdust logs.

**[#9818 · 2026-04-08 23:17 · Karl]**

**[#10283 · 2026-06-24 18:29 · Dane O]**

Well guys here it is, DM me if you have or know of anyone interested! 

After 2 years of development I'm putting the finishing touches on what I'm calling the "Aqueon" it's a domestic hot water heater replacement leveraging the m54-64-74 series miners with custom controller And 2 different heat-exchaners to differentially control your heat load to maximize uptime (dhw primary and furnace, radiant floor, or heat dump [for the lucky ones who live those places])

Don't need to replace your whole water tank, as it's considered a "retrofit" and could heat pretty much anything.

Web interface is locally hosted off the custom controller so all this can be run without any additional servers or setup.

Could have 5 of them ready by heating season for anyone who is interested in the most "plug and play" hydro system I could dream up!

Lots of features I don't want to textwall into here but let me know or DM me if you (or someone you know) would be interested!

🤯4

❤1
❤‍🔥1
👍1
👏1

**[#10285 · 2026-06-24 18:56 · Travis Bitkle]**

How does this connect to the domestic water side?

**[#10286 · 2026-06-24 19:26 · Dane O]**

3/4" shark bite fittings (or push connect) for both your DHW and whatever heating circuit (secondary) loop you want.

Can work as a standalone hot water heater, but could replace your furnace as well when your water heater is at capacity it would heat your house with the standard 24vac thermostat call for heat, cycling the miner on or switching from the dhw loop when satisfied.

🔥5

**[#10290 · 2026-06-25 18:28 · Travis Bitkle]**

I guess my question is more how you are circulation water into the water heater tank and that configuration.

**[#10291 · 2026-06-25 18:49 · Dane O]**

Circulation pump...

Depending on the homeowner and integration, some people tie it directly into their current hot water recirculation system (keeps hot water at the furthest tap in a house)

Or add a short loop with a stainless (or brass) circulation pump, out the tank, through the built in HX, back into DHW tank. (Can achieve different outcomes based on where you pull from/dump into as that's site dependant)

Same with the secondary heat loop, this system is designed to tie into existing infrastructure (radiant manifold and pumps already installed or furnace plenum loop built)

Intention is you just set the desired set point temps and the system will run to keep those satisfied

Always happy to help facilitate any and all installations you can dream up...

👍4

**[#10435 · 2026-08-08 20:53 · Christopher]**

BIP-110 hit its mandatory signaling period today at block 961,632. 

When Ocean mined a non-signaling block, BIP-110 nodes rejected it and forked off the main chain. 

With less than 3% miner support, the BIP-110 chain now trails the canonical chain with virtually no hashrate behind it.

**[#10610 · 2026-10-05 11:22 · Benji]**

Hey 
Quick update from Austria 🇦🇹 on my hashrate heater build:
I've tested my S19 hydro conversions using the Zeus Mining conversion kits, and they're running solid at ~80°C chip temp with no restarts so far. Current flow temperature is around 52°C.
Next step in the coming days: adding a 600 L buffer tank. The plan:
Lower coil: miner glycol loop
Upper coil: heating water
Target flow temp: 55–60°C
Has anyone run a similar setup with two coils in the buffer? Happy to hear any tips or lessons learned!
Best regards,
Benji ✌️

**[#10615 · 2026-10-06 08:31 · Benji]**

Thanks for the info. It’s a different story with Bitmain miners; the original Bitmain Hydro miners aren't really suited for that kind of setup—temperatures rise very quickly! The modified S19s run very well, though, with chip temperatures of 80–81°C and a heating system flow temperature of 50°C.
